```python
        third_count = sum(1 for l in range(j + 1, n) if a[l] == middle)
        count += first_count * third_count
    else:
        # For k != 1, first_needed must be integer
        if middle % k == 0:
            first_val = middle // k
            third_val = middle * k
            
            # Count elements before j that equal first_val
            first_count = sum(1 for i in range(j) if a[i] == first_val)
            
            # Count elements after j that equal third_val
            third_count = sum(1 for l in range(j + 1, n) if a[l] == third_val)
            
            count += first_count * third_count

print(count)
```

This is still O(n²). Let me use a more efficient approach with precomputation:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For k = 1, we need to count triplets of equal elements
if k == 1:
    # Count frequency of each element
    from collections import defaultdict
    freq = defaultdict(int)
    
    # Count how many times each value appears
    for val in a:
        freq[val] += 1
    
    # For each value, if it appears count times, number of triplets is C(count, 3)
    total = 0
    for count_val in freq.values():
        if count_val >= 3:
            total += count_val * (count_val - 1) * (count_val - 2) // 6
    
    print(total)
else:
    # For k != 1
    count = 0
    
    # Precompute prefix counts (for values that can be first elements)
    prefix_count = {}
    
    # Process left to right
    for j in range(n - 1):  # j goes from 0 to n-2 (middle position)
        # prefix_count contains count of values seen so far
        # For middle at position j, we need first = a[j]/k, third = a[j]*k
        
        if a[j] % k == 0:
            first_val = a[j] // k
            third_val = a[j] * k
            
            # First elements before j: count of first_val in positions 0 to j-1
            first_count = prefix_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Third elements after j: we'll compute this later
            
            # Update prefix count for current element (for future middle positions)
            prefix_count[a[j]] = prefix_count.get(a[j], 0) + 1
    
    # Now we need to count third elements after each position
    # Let's process from right to left to count third elements
    suffix_count = {}
    for j in range(n - 1, 0, -1):  # j from n-1 down to 1
        # For middle at position j, we need third elements > j
        # But we already processed all positions, so let's rethink
        
    # Alternate approach: process each middle position and count
    count = 0
    prefix_count = {}
    
    for j in range(1, n - 1):  # j from 1 to n-2
        middle = a[j]
        
        if middle % k == 0:
            first_val = middle // k
            third_val = middle * k
            
            first_count = prefix_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Count third elements after j
            third_count = 0
            for l in range(j + 1, n):
                if a[l] == third_val:
                    third_count += 1
            
            count += first_count * third_count
        
        # Update prefix count with current element (this will be used for future positions)
        prefix_count[middle] = prefix_count.get(middle, 0) + 1
    
    print(count)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarp loves geometric progressions very much. Since he was only three years old, he loves only the progressions of length three. He also has a favorite integer k and a sequence a, consisting of n integers.

He wants to know how many subsequences of length three can be selected from a, so that they form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A subsequence of length three is a combination of three such indexes i1, i2, i3, that 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < i3 ≤ n. That is, a subsequence of length three are such groups of three elements that are not necessarily consecutive in the sequence, but their indexes are strictly increasing.

A geometric progression with common ratio k is a sequence of numbers of the form b·k0, b·k1, ..., b·kr - 1.

Polycarp is only three years old, so he can not calculate this number himself. Help him to do it.

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers, n and k (1 ≤ n, k ≤ 2·105), showing how many numbers Polycarp's sequence has and his favorite number.

The second line contains n integers a1, a2, ..., an ( - 109 ≤ ai ≤ 109) — elements of the sequence.

Output

Output a single number — the number of ways to choose a subsequence of length three, such that it forms a geometric progression with a common ratio k.

Examples

Input

5 2
1 1 2 2 4


Output

4

Input

3 1
1 1 1


Output

1

Input

10 3
1 2 6 2 3 6 9 18 3 9


Output

6

In the first sample test the answer is four, as any of the two 1s can be chosen as the first element, the second element can be any of the 2s, and the third element of the subsequence must be equal to 4.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b*k, b*k²

So we're looking for indices i < j < k such that:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b*k  
- a[k] = b*k²

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at position j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before j equal a[j]/k (this would be the first element)
   - How many elements after j equal a[j]*k (this would be the third element)

2. The total count for position j would be (count of elements before j that equal a[j]/k) × (count of elements after j that equal a[j]*k)

3. We need to be careful about division by k, especially when k = 1 or when a[j] is not divisible by k.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle special case when k = 1**: All elements should be equal for a geometric progression.

2. **For each position j (as middle element)**:
   - If a[j] = 0: then we need b = 0, so first element must be 0 and third element must be 0
   - If k = 1: we need all three elements to be equal
   - Otherwise: we need first element = a[j]/k and third element = a[j]*k

3. **Count efficiently**:
   - Precompute counts of elements to the left of each position
   - Precompute counts of elements to the right of each position

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

# Handle the case when k = 1 - all elements must be equal
if k == 1:
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for x in a:
        freq[x] = freq.get(x, 0) + 1
    
    # For each element that appears at least 3 times, we can choose C(freq[x], 3) combinations
    for x in freq:
        if freq[x] >= 3:
            count += freq[x] * (freq[x] - 1) // 2 * (freq[x] - 2) // 2
    print(count)
else:
    # For general case k != 1
    # We'll process each middle position j
    left_count = {}
    right_count = {}
    
    # Initialize right_count with all elements
    for x in a:
        right_count[x] = right_count.get(x, 0) + 1
    
    # Process each position as middle element
    for j in range(n):
        # Current middle element is a[j]
        current = a[j]
        
        # Calculate what the first element should be (b)
        # We need b such that b * k = current, so b = current / k
        # This requires current % k == 0
        if current % k == 0:
            first_val = current // k
        else:
            first_val = None
        
        # Calculate what the third element should be (b * k^2)
        third_val = current * k
        
        # If we have a valid first value, add the product of left and right counts
        if first_val is not None:
            # Add count of first_val to the left of position j
            left_count_val = left_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Subtract current from right_count if it's in right_count
            right_count_val = right_count.get(third_val, 0)
            
            count += left_count_val * right_count_val
        
        # Update left_count for the current position (we'll use it for future middle positions)
        left_count[current] = left_count.get(current, 0) + 1
        
        # Remove current from right_count (as we processed it)
        right_count[current] = right_count.get(current, 0) - 1
        if right_count[current] == 0:
            del right_count[current]
    
    print(count)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio `k`.

A geometric progression with ratio `k` has the form: `b, b*k, b*k^2`

So we need to find triplets (i, j, l) where i < j < l and:
- `a[i] = b`
- `a[j] = b*k` 
- `a[l] = b*k^2`

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at position j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before position j equal to `a[j] / k`
   - How many elements after position j equal to `a[j] * k`

2. The total count for position j would be: (count of elements before j with value `a[j]/k`) × (count of elements after j with value `a[j]*k`)

3. We need to be careful about:
   - Division by k: when k=1, we need to handle it specially
   - Negative numbers: we need to ensure we're doing proper division
   - Zero: if any term is zero, it affects the progression

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle edge cases**: 
   - If k = 1, then progression is `b, b, b` - all elements are equal
   - If any element is 0, then the progression can only have 0,0,0

2. **For each middle element**:
   - Calculate what the first element should be: `current / k`
   - Calculate what the third element should be: `current * k`
   - Count valid pairs

3. **Optimization**: Precompute prefix and suffix counts to avoid recalculating for each position.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Count total subsequences
result = 0

# We'll use prefix and suffix counts to optimize
# prefix[i][val] = count of val in positions 0 to i-1
# suffix[i][val] = count of val in positions i+1 to n-1

# Special case: k = 1
if k == 1:
    # All elements must be equal
    count = {}
    for x in a:
        count[x] = count.get(x, 0) + 1
    
    # For each value, number of ways to choose 3 from count[x] elements
    for val, cnt in count.items():
        if cnt >= 3:
            result += cnt * (cnt - 1) * (cnt - 2) // 6
else:
    # For k != 1, we need to be careful with division and zero
    # We'll go through each position as the middle element
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - count of each value in positions after i
    suffix = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        suffix[i] = {}
        if i < n - 1:
            for val, cnt in suffix[i + 1].items():
                suffix[i][val] = cnt
        suffix[i][a[i]] = suffix[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position as the middle element
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        current = a[i]
        
        # What should be the first element? current / k
        # What should be the third element? current * k
        
        first_needed = current / k if k != 0 else 0
        third_needed = current * k
        
        # Check if first_needed is an integer
        if first_needed != int(first_needed):
            continue
            
        first_val = int(first_needed)
        
        # Check if current is divisible by k
        if current % k != 0:
            continue
            
        # Look for first element before position i
        first_count = 0
        for j in range(i):
            if a[j] == first_val:
                first_count += 1
        
        # Look for third element after position i
        third_count = 0
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if a[j] == third_needed:
                third_count += 1
        
        result += first_count * third_count

print(result)
```

Wait, I see some issues with the above code. Let me fix it with a cleaner approach:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

result = 0

# Handle k = 1 case separately
if k == 1:
    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)
    for val, cnt in count.items():
        if cnt >= 3:
            result += cnt * (cnt - 1) * (cnt - 2) // 6
else:
    # For k != 1, we need to be careful about division
    # Precompute suffix counts
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Fill suffix counts from right to left
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = {}
        if i < n - 1:
            for val, cnt in suffix_count[i + 1].items():
                suffix_count[i][val] = cnt
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position as middle element
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        current = a[i]
        
        # First element should be current / k, third should be current * k
        # Check if current is divisible by k
        if current % k != 0:
            continue
            
        first_val = current // k
        third_val = current * k
        
        # Count occurrences of first_val before position i
        first_count = 0
        for j in range(i):
            if a[j] == first_val:
                first_count += 1
        
        # Count occurrences of third_val after position i
        third_count = suffix_count[i + 1].get(third_val, 0)
        
        result += first_count * third_count

print(result)
```
